With Alt held, `matchesKey` reads a letter from `event.code` (`KeyO`), so Option's own character on a Mac (ø) doesn't matter. The plan's Call said it is matched on `event.key === '.'`, which was the Mod+. wording; it no longer applies. AltGr is guarded: on Windows AltGr is Ctrl+Alt, so a layout whose AltGr+O types a letter (Polish ó) sends Ctrl+Alt+O. Off a Mac, `matchesKey` reads a Ctrl+Alt letter by the key it types: a match when `event.key` is the bound letter, in either case (so Dvorak's o, on the physical S key, works, as does Colemak's), even if the browser reports AltGraph, since no layout's AltGr types a plain letter and Chromium may report it for a left Ctrl+Alt on an AltGr layout; otherwise no match when the browser reports AltGraph; when `event.key` is a letter of a non-Latin script (Cyrillic щ, Greek ο), the physical key (`event.code`) decides, since there Ctrl+Alt+O is the shortcut and not AltGr; anything else (an accented Latin letter such as ó or ń, punctuation such as Hungarian AltGr's `}`, a dead key, or Dvorak's r on the physical O) doesn't match, so the key types. This covers `Mod+Alt+N` (New screenplay) and AltGr+N as well. The registry still calls `preventDefault` on a matched global key before asking whether it is enabled, which is unchanged. In the desktop app the View menu shows `CmdOrCtrl+Alt+O` without registering it, as every item does.
- **2026-10-29** — **When the strip goes with focus in it, focus goes to the editor** (Call 32): `StructureStrip.vue`'s `onBeforeUnmount` checks whether the strip holds focus and, if so, calls `editor.goto` at the cursor's own place (which shows the Source and focuses it, a cursor move to where it already is), so turning the strip off with its key, a switch to a Markdown document, focus mode, or a narrowed window never leaves focus on the page's body.
- **2026-10-29** — **The card, measured when its place changes; the focused block's card comes back** (Call 11, after Task 3's review): the card's place is measured when its block's id, `left`, or `width` changes, not on every parse that rebuilds the rows. When the pointer leaves a block, the card goes back to the focused block's (none if no block has focus); `blur` still takes it away.
- **2026-10-29** — **The Script pane publishes its top page only while the strip is on** (Call 29, after Task 3's review): `ScriptPane.vue`'s `publishTop` returns at once while `structureStrip` is off, so a scroll costs nothing for a strip that isn't there, and it publishes once when the setting turns on, so the marker is right at once.
- **2026-10-29** — **A row per section depth the script uses, then Scenes, then the ruler** (Call 2): `structureStrip` makes rows only for the depths of `STRIP_DEPTHS` (1 to 3) that have a section, in order, so a script with `#` and `###` gets two section rows and no empty `##` one; `####` and deeper are never drawn, though their scenes are.
- **2026-10-29** — **Fit to width, no zoom** (Call 5): every block is placed in percentages of the whole axis, and each row clips, so the strip never scrolls sideways and the whole script is always on screen. A short scene in a long script can be only the 2 px minimum wide; its name is still on the block and in the card. A zoom that scrolls is in `docs/DEFERRED.md`.
- **2026-10-29** — **The strip edits nothing** (Call 13): no drag, no rename, no menu on a block. The only transaction it makes is `editor.goto`'s cursor move, so undo, the file, the printed pages, and the recovery copy never change for it. Reordering stays with the Outline and Cards; dragging blocks is in `docs/DEFERRED.md`.
- **2026-10-29** — **A script with no scenes shows one line** (Call 17): "No scenes yet. Each scene heading you write shows here." (`NO_SCENES`, `strip/labels.ts`), in place of the rows and the ruler, with no blocks to focus; the first scene heading brings the rows.
- **2026-10-29** — **Read-only tabs show the strip, and going there works** (Call 18): `editor.goto` moves only the cursor, which a read-only tab allows, so the strip needs no read-only case of its own; `strip.spec.ts` checks it in a second tab of the same script, which opens read-only: its strip goes to a scene and the tab stays read-only.
- **2026-10-29** — **No storylines and no "color by" in the strip** (Call 21): the only color is the scene's own color note. Storyline rows, a "color by" choice, and whether the script map (Phase 2 design §14) is still wanted beside the strip are Plan E's to decide (strip design §10), with storylines themselves.
- **2026-10-29** — **The strip is v0.15.0, and Plans E to H move down one** (Call 22): E (Seeing the script) is v0.16.0, F (the Beat Board) v0.17.0, G (Reader notes) v0.18.0, and H (Reference) v0.19.0; the letters stay, so the Phase 2 design still reads (its §22). The Markdown PDF and import releases queued after the strip may move them down again when they are planned.
- **2026-10-29** — **Plan D first** (Call 23): the branch started from `main` at v0.14.0 (`c8bf4a9`), with spellcheck already merged, so no merge was needed, and the strip's command and Settings edits sit after spellcheck's. That is also why its key is Mod+Alt+O (Call 31).
- **2026-10-29** — **e2e turns the strip on through the stored setting** (Call 24): `strip.spec.ts` and the strip's long-task test in `script.spec.ts` start with `drafterDatabase([...sampleSeenRows, { key: 'structureStrip', value: true }])` as their `storageState`, so only the tests about the controls use the controls. With the strip off by default, the existing axe tests see no change.

## Markdown PDF (`@drafter/render-pdf`, `apps/web`)

- **2026-10-29** — **Inter Tight's four static styles, from Google Fonts v9** (Calls 2, 36): Regular 400, Italic 400, SemiBold 600, and SemiBold Italic 600, version 3.004, as the CSS2 API serves them as TTF from `fonts.gstatic.com/s/intertight/v9/`, the brand's source, unmodified, in `packages/render-pdf/fonts/inter-tight/` with Google Fonts' `OFL.txt` and a SHA-256 table that `document-fonts.test.ts` checks. SemiBold is byte for byte the brand's file, so strong text and headings reuse it; Inter proper is not added. Code is Courier Prime Regular, the file already beside them. The faces are `text`, `textItalic`, `textBold`, `textBoldItalic`, and `code` (`DOCUMENT_FONT_FILES`).
- **2026-10-29** — **A document's widths come from the font's own advances, as pdf-lib's** (technical design §5.2): `fontMeasure` lays a string out with fontkit and sums each glyph's `advanceWidth × size / unitsPerEm`, which is what pdf-lib's `widthOfTextAtSize` computes, so what the layout measures is what is drawn: ligatures count as one glyph, and there is no kerning, since pdf-lib draws none. Each face caches a string's advance in font units, so a string is laid out once whatever the size. `has` is fontkit's `hasGlyphForCodePoint`.
- **2026-10-29** — **Inter Tight has all three bullets** (Call 6): •, ◦, and ▪ are in Regular (`document-measure.test.ts` records it), so the bullets by depth are •, ◦, ▪, and the fallback •, –, · isn't needed.
- **2026-10-29** — **The indent stops growing at a quarter of the text width** (Call 31): a quote or list level that would leave the text narrower than a quarter of the text area adds no indent (and a quote no bar of its own, which would cross its text), so a list ten deep in a quote five deep never pushes text off the page. `documentBlocks` takes the text width as a required second argument, so the layout always passes the paper's (468 pt on US Letter, 451.28 on A4) and no default can silently stand in for it.
- **2026-10-29** — **No depth of nesting overflows the stack**: `documentBlocks` walks blocks, inline marks, and the search for definitions on stacks of its own (arrays), never by recursion, so a quote, list, or emphasis ten thousand deep (`>` typed 5,000 times is one line) prints its text instead of throwing. Nothing is cut or flattened: every level is read, the indent stops at Call 31's cap, a list's markers all print, and a mark deep down keeps every style around it. The memory a walk takes grows with the depth, as the parser's tree already does.
- **2026-10-29** — **Space between blocks, where the design's numbers meet** (Call 4): a heading always has its own space before it (24, 20, 16, or 12 pt), even right under another heading, as the Preview's margins collapse; anything else after a heading has 6 pt; a rule has 12 pt before it and the block after it at least 12; a tight list has none between its items, or between an item's text and a list inside it, and a loose list 4 pt; an item's other blocks, and a quote's, have the 8 pt between text blocks.
- **2026-10-29** — **A list item's marker rides on its first block** (Call 6): an item that is empty, or begins with a list, a quote, or a rule, prints its marker on a line of its own first, so no marker is lost and none is drawn inside a quote's bar. Bullets go by how many lists hold the item, numbered ones included, as a browser counts them.
- **2026-10-29** — **Inline code in a heading keeps code's proportion** (Call 8): 10 pt beside 11 pt text, so 10⁄11 of the heading's size in a heading. Its color is the text's around it (muted in headings 5 and 6, link blue in a link).
- **2026-10-29** — **An image inside a link is part of the link** (Calls 9, 10): its `[Image: …]` is Italic as always, but blue and underlined with the link's address when the link has one, so what is clickable looks it; elsewhere it is muted.
- **2026-10-29** — **A reference whose definition is missing prints its text, plain**: the parser makes a reference node only when its definition exists (otherwise the brackets stay text, as typed), so this is only for a tree built some other way. The first definition of a label wins, as CommonMark has it.
- **2026-10-29** — **Lines break at spaces and after zero-width spaces, first fit, and are drawn exactly as measured** (Call 13): `wrapBlock` estimates a line from its words' widths (each run's part of a word measured whole), then measures each fragment (one per run of one style, spaces included) whole, as pdf-lib will draw it, and a line that comes out wider than its estimate gives back its last word, or, a lone word, its last character, until it fits. Inter Tight's contextual alternates make this real: "1x2" is wider drawn whole than its characters one by one, "->" narrower, so neither summing characters nor summing words can promise the drawn width; only measuring the drawn string can. A word's estimate sums its characters' widths and stops once they pass the room left on the line by a quarter of the line (a word drawn whole may be a little narrower than its characters, a few percent in Inter Tight, so a word more than a quarter line narrower whole would break by character rather than fit); only a word that could fit is measured whole, and one too wide goes straight to breaking by character. So every string measured for a line is at most about a line and a quarter of text, whatever the length of the word or paragraph, and the width cache holds single characters, words that fit a line, and each line's fragments: it grows with the document, not its square. (Measuring the rest of a 50,000-character word whole on each line it broke over took two minutes; it now takes milliseconds, and `document-lines.test.ts` checks the growth from 12,500 to 50,000 characters with `scaling`.) A fragment's `width` is always `measure.width` of its `text`.
- **2026-10-29** — **A word wider than the line starts a line of its own, then breaks at the character that overflows it** (Call 13): characters' widths are summed to find the edge, and the drawn check above pulls back a character if needed. Every line takes at least one character, so a character wider than the line itself (a narrow panel deep in a list) is drawn alone on its line, the one thing that may pass the edge, and the wrap always ends. Code and raw HTML break only this way, by character, at the panel's inner edge (or the line's), keeping every space; text drops the spaces at a break and at a line's end.
- **2026-10-29** — **Soft hyphens and zero-width spaces are dropped, never drawn** (§2.8): a soft hyphen (U+00AD) is invisible everywhere and is not a break, so no hyphen ever appears at a line's end (hyphenation is not in v1); a zero-width space (U+200B) leaves a break after the character before it, in text, and is otherwise dropped, since Courier Prime has no glyph for it and it has no width to draw. A no-break space (U+00A0) is part of its word. Control characters become spaces and a lone surrogate `?`, by `drawable`, now in `src/text.ts` and shared with the script renderer (Call 32; the script renderer keeps U+FFFD for lone surrogates). A lone surrogate isn't reported in `missing`, which names characters the writer can read; one a face lacks (CJK, emoji) is, once.
- **2026-10-29** — **A line's height is its block's leading, and its baseline sits `(leading − size) / 2 + 0.8 × size` below its top**: the text centred in its leading, its descenders in the bottom fifth of its size, the same for every face, so inline code and a marker share their line's baseline. A list item's marker is its block's first line's first fragment, right-aligned to `indent − 6`, so it sits on the first baseline of a code panel, raw HTML, or heading as of a paragraph. A code panel's 8 pt padding is in its first line's height (and ascent) and its last line's height, so the panel's rectangle runs from the first line's top to the last line's bottom. Every block gives at least one line (an empty paragraph or `#` alone places its marker or its space); a rule is one line its 0.5 pt thickness high, with no fragments.
- **2026-10-29** — **A wide ordered marker hangs into the left margin, by design** (§2.3): a marker's right edge is always at `indent − 6`, so the text of every item lines up whatever its number, and a number wider than the indent leaves it (at 11 pt, "10." starts about 1 pt left of the margin at the top level, x ≈ −0.9). The list's indent doesn't widen for long numbers. CommonMark allows a start of at most nine digits, and "999999999." (about 59 pt) starts at x ≈ −47, inside the page's 72 pt margin (x ≥ −72 from the text area's left edge); even the tenth digit a list counting on from there reaches ("1000000000.", x ≈ −52) stays inside, so a marker never leaves the paper; the layout's check that nothing lies outside the text area makes this one exception for markers (Task 4).
- **2026-10-29** — **Pages break in one walk, by four rules** (Calls 14 to 17): `paginate` places each block's lines in order and drops a block's space before at the top of a page (Call 14). A block that doesn't fit splits only with at least 2 lines on each page, pulling lines back to the next page if needed, so a block of 2 or 3 lines moves whole (Call 15). A heading, with any headings directly under it, moves to the next page unless what must follow it fits under it on this one (Call 16): the next block's first 2 lines, or all of it when it has 3 or fewer, since a block of 3 can't split and 2 lines fitting would still leave the heading alone at the foot. When a rule can't be kept, the lines fill the page as they come (Call 17): a heading with what must follow it taller than a page places its chain of headings line by line where they are, and the block after it fills only the page it shares with the chain's last heading; that block's first slice may be a single line, but from the next page on it splits by the usual rule, so it never leaves a single line at its end because of the heading. A block of 3 or fewer lines taller than a page also fills as it comes (moving it first would only leave a gap, since it splits anyway). Every page but an empty document's takes at least one line, so the walk ends, and a chain of headings is measured once, so it is linear.
- **2026-10-29** — **A split code panel keeps its padding only at its real top and bottom**: each page's part of a panel is one rectangle from its first line's top to its last line's bottom, so the part at a page's foot runs to the cut with no padding below its last line, and the part at the next page's top starts with none above, like a quote's bar, so the panel reads as one that continues over the page. Padding each part would make a line's height depend on where the page breaks, which pagination can't know before it places the line.
- **2026-10-29** — **Each quote has its own bar** (§2.3): `documentBlocks` numbers the quotes that draw a bar (`DocBlock.quotes`, beside `quoteBars`), so a bar runs unbroken through the space between one quote's blocks but two quotes one after the other (`> a`, a blank line, `> b`) draw two bars, as the Preview shows them. On each page a bar runs from the top of the quote's first line there to the bottom of its last.
- **2026-10-29** — **Page numbers** (Call 18): Inter Tight Regular 9 pt in the muted gray, the number alone, centred on the page by its measured width, its baseline 36 pt above the bottom edge, from 1 on the first page, only when there are two or more pages. A link's annotation rectangle is its fragment's width and its line's full height; its underline is 0.5 pt, 1.5 pt under the baseline. A rule is drawn through the middle of its 0.5 pt line, from its indent to the right margin. `layoutDocument` passes the paper's text width to `documentBlocks` and `wrapBlock` (468 pt on US Letter, 451.28 on A4).
- **2026-10-29** — **What is measured is what is drawn: one setting for OpenType features, without kerning** (technical design §5.2, controller's ruling): `layoutFeatures()` in `fonts.ts` is the one place a document's OpenType features are chosen: each font's defaults (ligatures such as fi, contextual alternates), with `kern` off. pdf-lib draws each glyph at its own advance and never kerning's positions, and the measure sums the same advances, so kerning was only work: off, no glyph or width changes, and laying text out takes about a third less time. `fontMeasure` lays strings out with it and `renderDocumentPdf` embeds each face with it, and `document-render.test.ts` checks pdfjs's drawn width of every string against the measured one, with strings ("1x2", "->") that Inter Tight's alternates change, so drawing with any other features fails it. It returns a new object each time because fontkit writes the features it applied back into the object it is given. Checked against what a writer types: fontkit's defaults leave `1/2`, `10/12`, and `29/10/2026` as typed (its fraction features act only around a fraction slash, U+2044, which a writer types to get a fraction), Courier Prime's ordinals (`ordn`) and superiors (`sups`) aren't on by default, and Inter Tight has neither; a test checks the three strings draw 3, 5, and 10 glyphs. Inter Tight's contextual alternates do change two things a writer types: `x` between digits draws as × ("1x2") and `->` as an arrow, as the Preview shows them in the same font (browsers apply them by default too); they are kept, like ligatures, and copied text still reads as typed.
- **2026-10-29** — **A document's PDF, drawn from the layout's items** (Calls 18, 19): pdf-lib draws each page's code panels first, so their text shows on them, then the text and lines in the layout's order, so the content stream reads in reading order. Each text item is one text operator at its baseline, placed with pdf-lib's own operators rather than `PDFPage.drawText`, which adds a new font name to the page's resources at every call; each page names each face once. Fonts are embedded as subsets under fixed names (`InterTight-Regular`, …, `CourierPrime-Regular`), only when a face is first drawn, so a document with no code carries no Courier Prime. Creator "Drafter", producer "Drafter, with pdf-lib", `updateMetadata: false`, the title (shown in the viewer's title bar) and `/Lang` only when given: the same tree, paper, title, and language give the same bytes. An empty tree is one blank page.
- **2026-10-29** — **Inter Tight's subsets are padded so viewers can draw them, or embedded whole** (controller's ruling): fontkit (`@pdf-lib/fontkit` 1.1.1, the subsetter pdf-lib uses) writes a subset whose glyph data is under 64 KB with a short-format `loca`, which stores offsets halved, but copies each glyph's data unpadded. Inter Tight's static files have hundreds of glyphs of odd length (Regular 515 of 2,541; Courier Prime none), so in a short document's subset every glyph after the first odd one pointed into the wrong place, and a viewer that draws from the font program (poppler, FreeType) printed blanks, while text extraction, which reads `/ToUnicode`, still found every word. `renderDocumentPdf` wraps the subset's `_addGlyph`, on each embedded font only, to pad a glyph of odd length with one zero byte, which the format allows. This reaches into pdf-lib's private embedder (`PDFFont.embedder.subset`), so `pdf-lib` (1.17.1) and `@pdf-lib/fontkit` (1.1.1) are pinned exactly in `@drafter/render-pdf`'s `package.json` (JSON has no comments; `docs/DEPENDENCIES.md` says why), and it never throws: each face's subset is first tried on a scratch document that is never saved, and if it isn't the shape the padding knows, that face is embedded whole (`subset: false`, about 300 KB) for the document, so the worst case is a bigger file, never a failed export or blank letters. `document-render.test.ts` reads every embedded subset back with fontkit and compares each glyph's outline and advance with the font's, and simulates the unexpected shape to check the fallback. The screenplay renderer doesn't need it (Courier Prime's glyphs are all of even length) and is unchanged, byte for byte.
- **2026-10-29** — **A link annotation per line, written as hex** (§2.6): a link is a `/Link` annotation with no border (`/Border [0 0 0]`) and a `/URI` action, one per line it covers: fragments of one link side by side on a line (a link with emphasis inside) join into one rectangle, and each page a link reaches gets its own. The address is checked again with `safeLink` before it becomes an annotation, and written as a hex string, since pdf-lib writes a `PDFString` as typed and an unbalanced parenthesis or a backslash in an address (both survive `URL.href`) would break the file.
- **2026-10-29** — **Rendering costs about as much as the layout** (§8, Call 34): fontkit's layout of a string through Inter Tight's substitution and positioning tables takes most of the time, and pdf-lib lays each drawn string out again to encode it. With kerning off (above), a 104-page document of unique text renders in about 2.6 seconds in Node on the maintainer's machine (3.5 with kerning). `document-budget.test.ts` renders one (its words drawn at random, so no measured width is reused) and logs the time, failing only past its hang guard; the 3-second budget is measured in the browser by Task 6's e2e test.
- **2026-10-29** — **The padding falls back in every case it can't handle** (Task 5's review): `evenGlyphs` installs nothing unless the subset has `_addGlyph` and the font's glyph data is a buffer class with a zero-filling `alloc` (fontkit's own, or Node's `Buffer`), so a subset it couldn't pad is never left unpadded; and when the scratch check passes but the document's own subset can't be padded, that font is taken out of pdf-lib's list before it is written (`unembed`, its private `fonts` array) and the face embedded whole. Both are tested by simulating the shapes.
- **2026-10-29** — **A Markdown PDF is its own request** (Call 35): `{ kind: 'markdown-pdf', source, paper, title, lang }`, beside a script's `{ kind, source, template }`; the reply carries `missing`, the characters printed as `?`, and `renderExport` answers `{ data, missing }` for every kind (empty for a script's). `exportScript('pdf')` sends it for a Markdown document with `settings.markdownPaper`, the title the file is named by (`markdownTitle`, else the file's base name), and `settings.spellLanguage`.
- **2026-10-29** — **The worker imports the parser on the first Markdown PDF** (Call 21): `createExporter` keeps three loads, each once and tried again after a failure: the script fonts, the document fonts, and `import('../markdown/parse')`. A script's export touches none of the document's (`export-compute.test.ts` counts the parser's module loads). In the build the export worker is one file (Vite's default worker format, IIFE), so the parser's code (with the decoder's table, some 80 KB of the worker's 1.3 MB) travels inside it but runs only on the first Markdown PDF; splitting it into a chunk of its own would mean `worker.format: 'es'` for every worker, which is deferred.
- **2026-10-29** — **Everything takes the entity decoder's own table** (found while building Call 21; widened in the Task 6 review): `decode-named-character-reference`, which the Markdown parser uses for `&amp;` and the like, has a `browser` build that decodes through `document.createElement`, and a worker has no `document`: the export worker failed with "document is not defined", and the Preview's worker had been failing the same way since it was added (the Preview fell back to the main thread, so it went unseen). A first fix, a Vite plugin for built workers only, missed `nuxt dev`, where module workers go through the page's own pipeline with the `browser` condition: there the Preview's worker still failed and a Markdown PDF never arrived (checked in Chromium against `nuxt dev`, before and after). So `nuxt.config.ts` aliases the package to its plain `index.js` for every environment: the page, the workers, dev, the build, and the desktop app. The cost is on the page's lazy parser chunk, which grows by about 29 KB (to 86 KB, 28 KB gzipped): it is fetched only with the first Markdown document, is precached anyway, and the decoding is the same. Accepted. The Markdown worker grows from 58 to 87 KB.
- **2026-10-29** — **The document fonts are fetched from the app's own folders, and precached** (Call 22): Inter Tight's four TTFs, render-pdf's own files (the ones the layout measures), are copied by `sync-assets.mjs` to `/fonts/inter-tight/` beside its licence, `check-build.mjs` requires them, and the service worker precaches them with the other `.ttf`s (about 1.2 MB, each well under Workbox's 2 MiB limit); Courier Prime Regular for code is the one at `/fonts/courier-prime/`. The interface's Inter Tight stays the hashed `woff2` it was.
- **2026-10-29** — **What each kind exports is one list** (Call 10's menus): `EXPORTS` in `storage/export.ts` (a script: PDF, plain text, Final Draft; a Markdown document: PDF, Markdown, plain text, the PDF first in both), read by the app menu's Export section and, through `exportApplies`, by the commands' `enabled`, so the palette and the desktop app's File menu follow it. The Export menu lists the same items in the same order.
- **2026-10-29** — **The missing characters in the toast** (Call 12): "Exported Notes.pdf. Some characters aren’t in Drafter’s fonts and print as ?: 漢, 字." (`MISSING_GLYPHS`), the first five in the order the renderer met them, joined with ", ".
- **2026-10-29** — **Paper for Markdown PDFs, after the spelling settings** (Call 20): `markdownPaper`, `'letter'` by default, `PAPERS = ['letter', 'a4']` (any other stored value is ignored), a select named US Letter and A4 with the design's description. It follows the spelling group (Spellcheck, Spelling language, Check notes and boneyard, My dictionary) rather than sitting between Spelling language and Check notes and boneyard, so the spelling settings stay together.
- **2026-10-29** — **A slow export says it is under way** (§8): an export rendered in the worker that takes more than half a second shows "Exporting Notes.pdf…" in the toast (a status, so a screen reader hears it once), held until the export ends, when "Exported …" or the error takes its place. A long document takes about 23 ms a page (103 pages in about 2.3 s in Chromium, Task 6's e2e test), and the editor stays usable meanwhile, since the work is in the worker. There is no Cancel; it is in DEFERRED.
- **2026-10-29** — **The Preview says why it can't show a document, and keeps its worker** (Task 6 review): a parse or a drawing that throws (a quote nested five thousand deep runs Chromium's drawing out of stack; Firefox and WebKit draw it) shows "This document is nested too deeply to preview. Your text is safe in the Source." (or, for any other failure, "The Preview couldn’t show this document. …") in the pane, as a status, instead of a broken pane. One text the worker can't parse is drawn on the main thread that once, and the worker is kept; a worker that fails is made again for the next edit, three times at most in a visit, after which long documents parse on the main thread after the same pause.
- **2026-10-29** — **No subset at all where one couldn't be taken back** (Task 6 review): `canPad` answers false when pdf-lib's private list of fonts to write isn't an array, since `unembed` couldn't then drop a subset found unpaddable; every face is embedded whole from the start (a bigger file, never blank letters).
- **2026-10-29** — **A long export shows how far it is** (Task 6b, the maintainer's request of 2026-10-29): render-pdf's renderers take an `onProgress` (`{ stage, done, total }`: `layout` by blocks, `draw` by pages, `save` at its start and end, since pdf-lib's save says nothing until it ends), and the export worker turns it into one fraction for the whole export (`workers/export-progress.ts`), each stage weighted by its measured share: a 100-page Markdown document 5 / 50 / 40 / 5 (prepare, layout, draw, save; measured 4%, 52%, 43%, 1.5%), a script 10 / 70 / 20 (parse and paginate as one step, draw, save; a 600-page screenplay measured 10%, 69%, 21%). Fetching and parsing, and saving, can't say how far they are: the worker sends null, and the bar moves without a number. At most one message each 100 ms (some ten a second), except a switch between known and unknown, and the last, 1, which are always sent; plain text and Final Draft send null, then 1. The worker's messages are `{ progress }` before the reply. Recorded in Chromium: a 103-page document's bar went 13, 17, 21, … 93, unknown while saving, then 100.
- **2026-10-29** — **The toast's bar** (Task 6b): under "Exporting The Call.pdf…" (shown after half a second, as before), a `role="progressbar"` named "Exporting The Call.pdf", `aria-valuemin` 0 and `aria-valuemax` 100, `aria-valuenow` the whole percent (rounded down, so 100 only when the file is there), and "42%" drawn beside it (hidden from screen readers, as the bar has it); while the worker can't say, no `aria-valuenow`, `aria-valuetext` "Under way", and a short piece of the bar sweeps across. A screen reader hears the start (the status) and the end ("Exported …"), and "Export 50% done." in a polite live region as each quarter is passed, the highest one passed only, never a quarter passed before the bar showed, never beside the end (the last value, 1, says no "75% done" next to "Exported …"), and never the bar's every step; the hidden text is cleared 2 seconds after it is set, once it has been said, so no stale quarter stays in the toast. The track has a 1 px edge in `--faint` (3:1 on the toast's `--chrome-2`), since its own `--chrome` is barely different from the toast. With `prefers-reduced-motion: reduce` the fill has no transition and the unknown stage is the whole bar, still, in solid `--muted` (3:1 on the track in both themes; `tokens.test.ts` checks both pairs) rather than the gold of a known amount. Forced colors draw it in CanvasText and Highlight. The desktop app serves the same web build, so its toast is the same.
- **2026-10-29** — **An export is joined only by the same export** (Task 6 review, M5): `exportScript` joined any export of the same kind under way, so exporting B's PDF while A's was drawn gave B A's file. It now joins only when the kind, the script (its project's id), the text, the template, the Markdown paper, and the spelling language are all the same; anything else waits its turn and is its own. The paper and language a Markdown PDF uses are the ones it was asked with. The key it is joined by is taken when the export is asked for, while the text it exports is read when it starts (after any export ahead of it): so an export asked, then the text changed, then the same export asked again is not joined (the text differs) and runs twice, each with the text as it was when that run began.
- **2026-10-29** — **The rest of the design's Calls, as they landed** (Markdown PDF design §14; Task 7): margins of 1 inch all round, ragged right, never justified (Call 1); Inter Tight and Courier Prime embedded, never the standard PDF fonts, which have neither the characters nor the look (Call 3); a quote indented 16 pt with a 2 pt bar, its text in its own styles, not italic (Call 5); a code block on a light panel, wrapped at the character that meets the edge with nothing added, the fence's language ignored (Call 7); a link's address isn't printed beside its text, only made clickable (Call 11); the 100-page budget of 3 s is met in Chromium's worker (103 pages in 2.3 s) and in Node (104 pages in 2.5 s), and growth is checked by `scaling` (Calls 23, 34); the layout lives in `@drafter/render-pdf`'s `src/document/`, over its own `DocNode` type (Call 24), pure over a `Measure` that `fontMeasure` builds with fontkit (Call 25); the CLI's `drafter build` still takes only Fountain (Call 26, in DEFERRED); `export-pdf` is on for both kinds, the PDF first in a Markdown document's Export menu (Call 27, and the line on what each kind exports above); the PDF is untagged, with no bookmarks, for scripts and documents alike (Call 28, in DEFERRED); the release is v0.16.0 (Call 29, and the renumbering below). From the plan: `mdast-util-from-markdown` is a development dependency of `@drafter/render-pdf` at the app's range, `^2.1.0` (Call 30), and `scaling.ts` is copied into its tests with a `scalingAsync` variant, not imported across packages (Call 33).
- **2026-10-29** — **Two decisions of 2026-10-26 now give a Markdown document the PDF too**: "The app menu is the mark, and every item is a command" (whose Export section "lists only what the document kind exports", then the PDF for Fountain alone) and "Export is three commands" ("the PDF is on for a Fountain script"). Both rules stand, and a Markdown document now exports a PDF, so `export-pdf` is on for both kinds and the Export section lists, from `EXPORTS`, PDF, Markdown, and plain text for a document, and PDF, plain text, and Final Draft for a script.
- **2026-10-29** — **Plans E to H become v0.19.0 to v0.22.0** (Phase 2 design §22, the controller's ordering): the Markdown PDF took v0.16.0, and two releases go ahead of Plan E, v0.17.0 (guided first-script starters, export options, a better running time, and a Technical details page) and v0.18.0 (version history, with GitHub-style diffs). E (Seeing the script) is v0.19.0, F (the Beat Board) v0.20.0, G (Reader notes) v0.21.0, and H (Reference) v0.22.0; the letters stay, and the imports release (Highland, Fade In, and Markdown) will move them down once more when it lands. The Markdown PDF design's §13 table (v0.17.0 to v0.20.0) is kept as it was decided, with a line saying what changed.

## Getting a script out the door (v0.17.0)

- **2026-10-29** — **The guided starters talk to a newcomer, name each button and its mark, and teach Fountain's own marks** (out-the-door design §1.1, Call 1): two `.fountain` files beside Help's sample scripts, `guided-screenplay.fountain` and `guided-stage-play.fountain`, in Drafter's own words (clean room: no other application's template, sample, or tutorial was looked at). Each note is a `[[…]]` paragraph of its own after the example it explains, names its button as "the *Label* button" with the label as the bar shows it, and gives the mark in words or as typed ("The Scene button, marked INT., writes one for you"). A note never writes a region's own marks inside it (no `/*`, `*/`, or `[[`), so a note can't open a boneyard; the boneyard's note says "a slash and a star". The screenplay prints as one page and the play as two (Act One starts after the cast list's page break): short, since the notes do the teaching.
- **2026-10-29** — **Every element a note explains is that element, and every button a note names exists** (Calls 2 and 3): `starters.test.ts` writes out each starter's whole list of block kinds, lays each out with no diagnostics, and checks `buttonsNamed` (every "the X button", one or two words) against `barFor` of the starter's template: nothing named that the bar hasn't got, and every main button named.
- **2026-10-29** — **No deliberate Problem** (Call 4, overruled by the controller): the design had the screenplay's Your turn heading leave out its time of day, to meet the Problems tab on a mistake it explains. Dropped: a newcomer's first look is clean. Both starters open with no Problems (tested, and in e2e), the Your turn heading is complete (`INT. YOUR FIRST LOCATION - DAY`), and the first scene heading's note says why a heading has a time of day, and that the Problems tab points out one without.
- **2026-10-29** — **Two commands, in four places, with no key** (Call 5): `new-guided-screenplay` and `new-guided-stage-play` (File), titled New guided screenplay and New guided stage play, listed after the three New items in `COMMAND_DATA`, so in the palette, the Keyboard shortcuts page, the app menu's New section, and the desktop app's File menu after New Markdown document; and in Help → Sample scripts after the three tiny samples, below a separator, as Guided first screenplay and Guided first stage play (graduation-cap icon). The desktop menu's layout gained `also` for a section that lists another menu's commands by its own labels, so Help → Sample Scripts lists the two File commands, which stay in File too.
- **2026-10-29** — **A starter opens at the top, as a new, unchanged script of the writer's own, marked `starter`** (Calls 6 and 25): as a sample script does (`addScript` with its text as the baseline, then `open`), with `starter: kind` on the project (unindexed, no new database version) instead of `sampleScript`, and the cursor at 0, on the title page, where the lesson starts. `metSample` ignores a starter as it ignores a sample script, so the first New of a kind still opens with its annotated sample.
- **2026-10-29** — **The starters' command data is text-free** (Call 24): `editor/starter-kinds.ts` holds each starter's kind, command, title, and menu label, as `sample-kinds.ts` does for the samples; `commands/data.ts` and the app menu read it, and only `editor/starters.ts` (and so `builtin.ts`) imports the scripts' text, so the prerendered Keyboard shortcuts page doesn't carry them.
- **2026-10-29** — **The title page's note names Export options** (§1.2): it says Export options, at the end of the Export menu, can leave all of the title page, or all but the title and author, off a PDF. That dialog lands later in this release (Task 3), which ships with it.
- **2026-10-29** — **"Title and author only" keeps Title, Credit, Author or Authors, and Source, by the layout's own key aliases** (Call 7): `titlePageFor` filters the fields by the same `SLOTS` table the title page lays out with (case and spaces ignored), keeping the centered slots in the order they were written, and the title page's `span` and `raw`; it never changes the one it is given, and `'full'` is the same object. A title page left with only fields that don't print (Contact, Draft date) lays out as none, so the script still starts on page 1.
- **2026-10-29** — **PDF and plain text take both options; a Final Draft file takes the title page option and keeps its numbers as written** (Call 8, accepted by the controller): the worker cuts the title page before `paginate` and `toFdx` alike, and passes `sceneNumbers` to `paginate` only. Final Draft decides what it prints, and dropping a written number from a `.fdx` would lose it.
- **2026-10-29** — **`paginate`'s boolean stays** (Call 26): `sceneNumbers` is `boolean | 'none' | 'written' | 'all'`, `true` is `'all'`, `false` is `'none'`, and absent is the template's `enabled` (as `'all'`), so the CLI's `--scene-numbers` and `--no-scene-numbers` and every default export are byte-identical (compared with `cmp` on the samples and the layout fixtures, numbered and not).
- **2026-10-29** — **The options are per script, on the project record, never in the text or Settings** (Call 9, accepted by the controller): `Project.exportOptions` (unindexed, no new database version), read through `exportOptionsOf`, which takes a missing or unknown value (from a later version) as its default, one option at a time. They are kept through the script session's `keepOnProject`, which changes the copy the session holds and the stored one together (as `patch` does for the session's own fields), so a later save or recovery write can't drop them; nothing is dispatched to the editor, so the script isn't changed and Undo has nothing. A read-only tab keeps nothing. They stay in this browser: another browser, or the file opened elsewhere, starts with the defaults (keeping them in the metadata block is deferred).
- **2026-10-29** — **An Export options… dialog at the end of the Export menu, and in the palette; exports don't ask** (Call 10): command `export-options` (File, no key), after `export-treatment` in `COMMAND_DATA`, so the desktop app's File → Export lists it last by the existing `export-` prefix, under its own title (the submenu cuts only "Export as "). The toolbar's menu and the app menu's Export section end with a separator and the item; for a Markdown document the command is off and the item says why ("A Markdown document has no scene numbers or title page."). On in a read-only tab: the dialog shows both radio groups disabled with "Another tab has this script open, so its export options can’t be changed here." Each choice is kept the moment it's made; Done closes. The dialog is a native `<dialog>`, as the treatment dialog is. Number every scene's line says that a written number is kept and the rest are numbered by place, so two scenes can share a number (Task 2 review); numbering that never repeats is deferred.
- **2026-10-29** — **The export's toast names any option that isn't the default** (Call 11): "Exported The Call.pdf, with scene numbers as written and the title and author only." (`optionsPhrase`: "scene numbers as written" or "every scene numbered", then "the title and author only" or "no title page", joined by "and"). A Final Draft file's toast names only the title page, since its numbers are as written whatever the choice. The options are read in `run`, with the text, the template, and the name, after a switch under way has finished, so an export is never of one script with another's options; they are also part of the export join key, so an export asked for after a change of options is its own, never joined to one under way with the old ones.
- **2026-10-29** — **Minutes a page, per script, default 1 for every template, 0.5 to 3** (Call 12): `Project.timing.minutesPerPage`, read through `ratesOf` (`stats/timing.ts`), which takes a missing, non-finite, or out-of-range value (or one off its 0.1 step) as its default, one rate at a time. The running time is `runningTime(Math.round(pages × rate))`, with "at 1 minute a page" after it in Stats; the field's hint says plays often run longer.
- **2026-10-29** — **Spoken pace per script, default 150 words a minute, 100 to 220 in steps of 5** (Call 13, kept per script by the controller): `Project.timing.wordsPerMinute`, beside the page rate. Both rates are kept as export options are (Call 9): through `keepOnProject`, whose updater runs in its turn and never marks the script changed; a read-only tab keeps nothing, and a sample's rates go with its first Save. `parseRate` refuses `''`, `0`, `-1`, `1,2`, `abc`, `10`, `0.55`, and `152`, and the field says what's allowed (`aria-invalid`, `aria-describedby`) while the last good rate stays: "Enter a number from 0.5 to 3, in tenths, such as 1.2." (the tenths named, since 0.55 is in range but off the step; Task 4 review) and "Enter a whole number from 100 to 220, in steps of 5." After a rate is kept, the field shows what `setRate` gives back, the script's rates as stored, so it never shows one that wasn't kept.
- **2026-10-29** — **Action is 1.5 seconds a printed line, counted from the text at its role's width, and fixed** (Calls 14 and 15): `blockTiming` counts each source line of an action block as `ceil(printed characters / width)`, at least 1, at the width of `resolveRole(template, block, null)` (no built-in template has an `afterRole` rule; one that did would time its action at the unconditioned role's width). Printed characters leave out emphasis marks and notes. Dialogue and lyric words are spoken (inline notes and note-only lines left out); headings, cues, parentheticals, transitions, centered text, notes, the boneyard, sections, synopses, and the title page take no time. `ACTION_SECONDS_PER_LINE` is the one place to tune it. **Dual dialogue counts once** (Task 4 review): the two speeches of a `^` pair are said at the same time, so `TimingTally` counts the pair as its longer speech, pairing as the layout does (a `^` with the speech just before it, when nothing that prints comes between and that speech isn't already in a pair; otherwise it prints alone and counts in full). The layout pairs across a screenplay's section (which prints nothing); the tally doesn't, so the scene index's scenes, preamble, and sections' openings add up to the script's total exactly (a property test). **A speech is what the layout groups as one** (Task 5 review): its cue and the parentheticals, dialogue, and lyrics right after it. A note or boneyard on a line of its own after a blank line, or a synopsis, ends it, as it ends the layout's (what follows prints on its own, so a lyric after it is no one's words), while leaving it free to pair with a `^` after it. A property test holds each scene's `spokenBy`, plus the words outside any speech, to every word spoken in it.
- **2026-10-29** — **The Outline's rows show the page-rate time only; the strip's card adds the estimate; names say both in words** (Call 16): `rowTime(eighths, rates)` in `outline/labels.ts` gives a row's time at the script's minutes a page (`''` without pages), so a scene's meta is `p. 12 · 1 3/8 pp · 1 min 25 s` and a section's `5 scenes · 12 pp · 12 min` (the sum of its scenes' eighths at the rate; the section's opening before its first scene has no length, as before). `sceneName` and `sectionName` take the rates (defaulting to `DEFAULT_RATES`, so a caller that passes none reads a minute a page) and add the time in words ("about 1 minute 25 seconds"). The strip's card adds the time after the length and a line "Estimate from the dialogue: 1 min 10 s" (from `Scene.timing` or `Section.timing` at the script's spoken pace); the estimate needs no pages, so it shows before the layout comes, and a block with nothing to time (a heading alone, a section with no scenes) has no estimate line, as the card leaves out any line it has nothing for. The card is hidden from screen readers, so `blockName` says the estimate too ("estimate from the dialogue: about 1 minute 10 seconds"), before the goal. **A section's estimate is its scenes only** (Task 5 review): what sits in its opening before its first scene (a synopsis, a note, a line of action) is in the script's total but in no section's, as its length isn't either, so a section's time and estimate measure the same thing. Both views, and Stats, read the open script's rates through one shared computed, `useRates()` (`stats/timing.ts`), so a rate set in Stats changes every time at once; it keeps the same object while both numbers stay the same, so a save, which changes the project, doesn't make every time on screen work itself out again. How a time reads (`shortTime`, `timeWords`, `pageSeconds`, the labels' words, `Rates` and `DEFAULT_RATES`) is in `stats/time-format.ts`, which imports nothing of the app's, so `outline/labels.ts` and `strip/labels.ts` don't pull in the script session (a test reads their imports).
- **2026-10-29** — **Short times read to 5 seconds under ten minutes, then to the minute** (Call 17): `shortTime` ('10 s', '1 min 25 s', '12 min', '1 h 5 min') and `timeWords` ('about 1 minute 25 seconds', for accessible names), ready for the Outline and the strip. Anything above nothing is at least 5 seconds, so a scene with a few words never reads "0 s"; nothing at all is "0 s" and "no time". The script's totals keep `runningTime`'s "About 98 min".
- **2026-10-29** — **Timing is counted per block in `@drafter/outline`, inside the scene index's pass, and per parse in `countScript`** (Call 18): `sceneIndex` adds each block's `blockTiming` in the loop it already makes over a scene's blocks, so each `Scene` and `Section` (the sum of its scenes; nothing for a section with none) carries `timing: { spoken, actionLines }`, with no extra parse and no layout. `countScript(doc, layout, template)` sums it over every block of the parse, the preamble included, remembered by the parse as its words are (asked again with another template, it counts again). `Counts.minutes` is gone: the running time is figured from `pages` at the script's rate where it's shown. A scaling test (n and 4n) keeps the scene index linear. Both add blocks through `TimingTally`, in that same pass. For a later per-character view (who speaks how long, and in which scenes), each `Scene` also keeps `spokenBy`, the words each speaker says in it (both sides of a dual pair in full), made in the same loop, so that view reads the scene index and counts nothing again (Task 4 review: the per-block figures alone weren't enough, since they don't say whose a speech is).
- **2026-10-29** — **Technical details: plain words first, then the specifics, in the design's ten parts, and only what the code says** (Call 19): `about/tech.ts` holds `TECH_SECTIONS`, each with `plain` then `details`; `pages/docs/technical.vue` draws them as a help page (`help-technical`, `/docs/technical`, last in `HELP_PAGES`, so it is in the Help index, the docs bar, the app menu's Help with `i-lucide-cpu`, the palette, About Drafter, and the desktop app's Help as "Technical Details"). The design's example sentence for a keystroke ("reads the script again, but only the part around the letter") isn't what the code does: `reparse` parses the full text on every change (technical design §2.7), so the page says Drafter reads the whole script again, and why that's fast enough. The page names no versions.
- **2026-10-29** — **Five diagrams as data, drawn as HTML lists on the page and as Mermaid in `docs/technical-details.md`** (Call 20, the controller's ruling): the packages in layers, a keystroke, storage, export, and a release. `components/TechDiagram.vue` draws a flow as a `<figure>` with a caption and an `<ol>` of boxes with CSS arrows (`aria-hidden`), and the packages as an `<ol>` of layers (the top one first), each a `<ul>` of boxes whose hidden text says what the package uses; theme tokens only. `about/mermaid.ts` writes a flow as `flowchart TD` and the packages as `flowchart BT` with an edge from each used package to its user (so the parser is at the bottom, as on the page, whose arrows between layers point up); labels escape `"`, `<`, and `>` as Mermaid entities. `technicalMarkdown` writes the whole page, and `test/tech.test.ts` holds `docs/technical-details.md` to it with a file snapshot (`-u` rewrites it). The desktop app uses none of the packages (it serves the web build), so `drawnPackages` leaves it out of the diagram, and the text says so. `TechBlock` gained `link` (a repository path, a GitHub link on the page and a relative one in the Markdown) and `dependencies` (where the list goes), and an `Inline` reads `**strong**` as well as backticks. **The package diagram draws direct uses only** (the controller's ruling after the Task 6 review): `directUses` keeps a use only when no other use of the same package already reaches it (the transitive reduction), so Mermaid draws 25 lines instead of 45, and the caption says that each use shown is a direct one and a package also uses whatever those use; a test holds every package's reach in the reduced graph to its reach through `uses`. On the page, each box shows its direct uses as small muted text under its name ("uses @drafter/layout and @drafter/templates"), visible, not only to a screen reader. **Storage is a fan, not a chain** (Task 6 review): the editor's text goes to the recovery copy, to Save, and to snapshots each in its own time, so the flow's `fan` draws the first box, one arrow, and the other three side by side (Mermaid: an edge from the first step to each). A box's label and detail have a visually hidden ": " between them, so a screen reader doesn't run them together. The desktop part says the Mac app checks for a new version with its own small check (`electron-updater` installs only a signed app), and the release part that only the Linux and Windows jobs gate a release.
- **2026-10-29** — **Dependencies by name, one plain line each, checked against every `package.json`** (Call 21): `DEPENDENCY_NOTES` must name exactly the third-party `dependencies` of `apps/*` and `packages/*` (missing and stale both fail), and `PACKAGES` each package's exact `@drafter/*` runtime dependencies. The page links `docs/DEPENDENCIES.md` for the reasons and the Third-party licences page, which says it has the spellchecker's, the dictionaries', and the fonts' licences (what it does have). Development tools get one sentence.
- **2026-10-29** — **This release is v0.17.0; version history is v0.18.0, the imports v0.19.0, and Plans E to H v0.20.0 to v0.23.0** (Call 22, as the controller ordered it since the design): the design moved Plans E to H to v0.18.0–v0.21.0 with the imports next; since then version history was put ahead of the imports, so the Phase 2 design §22 (a decided paragraph, a `Getting a script out the door — v0.17.0` heading with **Status:** built, and the renumbered headings), its §26, the architecture's §13, the README's status line and roadmap, and the out-the-door design §7.1 (a "Since" line under its table, kept as decided) all give that order. The letters stay.
- **2026-10-29** — **Plan E takes Scaffold, and only Scaffold is called that** (the maintainer, 2026-10-29): an X-ray-style panel of who is in the current scene and where, with a page for each character and location, built on the Stats tab (§16) and this release's per-scene `timing` and `spokenBy`, is in Plan E's scope and done-when in the Phase 2 design, and in the architecture's Phase 2 list. Expand from outline is no longer described as scaffolding (architecture §10, technical design §7); the app's own words never used it.
- **2026-10-29** — **The plan's own Calls, as they landed** (Calls 23 and 27 to 33): `main` at v0.16.0 was merged before Task 1 (Call 23); the options travel in the export request, read in `run` with the text and template (Call 27, in Call 11's line); the Export menu ends with a separator and Export options…, last in the desktop app's File → Export (Call 28, in Call 10's line); `Counts.minutes` is gone (Call 29, in Call 18's line); a rate out of range is refused in the field and one stored out of range reads as its default (Call 30, in Calls 12 and 13's lines); labels take the rates as an optional last argument, a minute a page and 150 words a minute by default (Call 31, in Call 16's line); `docs/technical-details.md` is a Vitest file snapshot (Call 32, in Call 20's line); and Technical details is the last help page, with `i-lucide-cpu`, and "Technical Details" in the desktop Help menu (Call 33, in Call 19's line).

## Version history (`@drafter/compare`, `@drafter/history`, `apps/web`)

- **2026-10-30** — **Changed lines are paired by position, kept when alike** (Call 23): in a change that replaces lines, removed line i is paired with added line i when `lineSimilarity`, the Dice coefficient over the two lines' words (a run of letters, digits, `'` and `’`; spaces and punctuation don't count; a repeated word counts as often as both have it), is at least `PAIR_MIN` = 0.4. Case counts, as it does in the word diff, so a line turned to capitals is unpaired and gets no word marks.
- **2026-10-30** — **The word diff** (Call 24): `diffWords` tokens are a run of letters, digits, `'` and `’`; a run of whitespace; or any other single character (by code point). `diffLines` diffs the tokens; a removal and the addition after it are one change, and an unchanged run of only whitespace, or of one or two characters, between two changes is folded into them. Null when either side has more than `MAX_TOKENS` = 2,000 tokens.
- **2026-10-30** — **Folds keep three lines of context and open 200 at a time** (Call 26): `foldRows` folds an unchanged stretch within the range when it is longer than 2 × `CONTEXT` (6) lines, keeping `CONTEXT` lines next to each change in the range and the range's first row, a group's heading, even when the first change is far below it (no other context at the range's own edges); a stretch in a range with no change folds whole but for that first row. The plan's property "no fold is shorter than 2 × CONTEXT + 1" reads the stretch as the fold: a stretch of 7 between two changes folds 1 line, as a unified diff does. `openFold` gives the first `FOLD_STEP` = 200 rows and a fold of the rest; the view decides where to open from.
- **2026-10-30** — **Groups tile the rows; each line is in the group of its own heading** (Calls 21 and §5.2): `groupRows` cuts at a row whose line is an anchor of its side (older for a removed row, newer otherwise); a removed anchor paired with an added anchor is one renamed group and the added row doesn't cut. **A change that crosses an anchor is cut there:** given the same anchors, `diffRows` lays such a change out a section at a time (the removed and added lines before its first anchor; then each removed section and the added section whose heading its heading is paired with, a rename; a section whose heading isn't paired on its own, removed before added), so a line that replaced one above a heading stays in that heading's group, and pairs stay in order. Without anchors a change is still its removed rows then its added ones. A group's `changes` lists every change it holds a row of, so a change cut by a heading is in each of its groups (consecutive ones), and a group lists a change exactly when it has added or removed rows. `pairRows` puts a row beside its pair when the pair is in the range, unpaired rows side by side by position between pairs, so a range holding part of a change still lines up. The opening group's title is empty; the view names it. `markdownAnchors` takes ATX headings of levels 1 to 3 (up to three spaces in, a space after the marks, closing marks dropped; an empty heading such as `#` or `## ##` has an empty title) and skips fenced code (``` or ~~~, closed by the same character at least as long).
- **2026-10-30** — **A new package, `@drafter/history`, for what needs a parse** (version-history design §11, Call 35): `scenePrints`, `hash53`, `sketchOf`, `similarity`, `sameScene`, `deletedScenes`, `sceneVersions`, `putBackPlace`, `putBackChanges`, and `sceneAnchors`, pure and tested in Node. It depends on `@drafter/fountain`, `@drafter/outline`, and `@drafter/templates` and nothing else (a `docs/DEPENDENCIES.md` row came first). `sceneAnchors` returns its own `Anchor` (`{ line, title }`), the same shape as `@drafter/compare`'s, so `groupRows` takes it without the package depending on Compare.
- **2026-10-30** — **The 53-bit hash is written for Drafter** (Call 4): two 32-bit lanes, each taking every UTF-16 code unit by xor and a multiply, finished by xor-shift and multiply rounds; 21 bits of one and 32 of the other make 14 hex digits. Its multipliers are mathematical constants (the golden ratio's fraction, and the square roots of 2 and 3), so nothing is owed to a hashing library. A shingle's 32 bits are one lane of the same. The tests pin three values: a change to the hash must bump `PRINT_VERSION`.
- **2026-10-30** — **Shingles of three printed words, a bottom-k sketch of 64** (Call 5): the words of a scene's printed blocks after its heading (not notes, boneyard, synopses, or sections), lowercased, by the scene index's word pattern; a scene under five words (too few for three shingles) adds its word pairs, and a scene of one word is that word (the controller's rulings), so "They drive." grown to "They drive in silence." under its heading still shares the pair "they drive" and matches. **No single words beside pairs** (the second ruling): with them, "They drive." and "They kiss." under one heading shared "they", a third of the smaller's shingles, and read as one scene, so cutting either was never listed; now they share nothing, nor do "She waits." and "She leaves.". The cost: "Silence." grown to "Silence falls." shares nothing (a word against a pair), and reads as two scenes. `PRINT_VERSION` is 2, so cached prints of version 1 are read as misses and made again; a scene with none has an empty sketch. `similarity` takes J from the 64 smallest hashes of the two sketches together, which are the 64 smallest of both scenes' shingles, or all of them when both scenes fit in their sketches (then exact). C is estimated straight from the sketches, not derived from J: up to τ, the larger scene's largest sketch value (the smaller of the two largest when both sketches are full), every hash of either scene is in its sketch, so C is the share of the smaller's hashes up to τ found in the larger's (0 when it has none up to τ). Property tests, seeds fixed: J within 0.15 of the truth over random scenes of 150 to 600 words; C within 0.35 over a scene of 60 to 200 words partly inside one of 150 to 400 more (its sample is about 64 × smaller / larger hashes, so the spread is wider than J's; 99% of 3,000 runs were within 0.25).
- **2026-10-30** — **Call 6's thresholds, tuned against the fixtures, gain a fourth rule** (the controller's ruling): equal hash; or the same heading key and (either empty or C ≥ 0.25 with at least two shingles in common, or the one of a one-shingle scene: `MATCH.shared`); or J ≥ 0.5; **or, whatever the headings, C ≥ 0.75 where the smaller version has at least 8 shingles** (`MATCH.contained`, `MATCH.containedMin`). The fixtures (`Last Ferry`, 12 scenes in two acts; `Night Fares`, five scenes under `INT. CAR - NIGHT`; `The Lantern Room`, a play) showed a scene merged into another under the other's heading at J 0.44 and C 0.93, both scenes merged under a new heading at J 0.44 and 0.47, and the half of a split scene under a new heading at J 0.41 and C 0.95: Call 6 alone listed each as deleted though its text is all still there. Every related pair has C ≥ 0.86; no two different scenes of the fixtures (24, the five car scenes and a scene sharing one line with another among them) have J above 0.08 or C above 0.20 (0.16 with C derived from J), so 0.75 leaves a wide margin both ways. With it, all 13 labelled pairs and all 276 pairs of different scenes are classified right. What it lets through: a scene cut hard under a new heading, whose remnant is most of a smaller scene now, reads as still there, as rule 2 already reads one cut hard under its own heading. **Rule 2 takes either side empty** (the controller's ruling after review): a heading written first and filled in later, or a scene emptied to its heading, is the same scene, so the empty version isn't listed as deleted. **C from the sketches, not from J** (after review): derived from the bottom-64 J, a short scene merged into a long one read as the same only 58% to 86% of the time; estimated directly, in 200 tries each, a 15-word scene merged into a 150-word one reads as the same 200 times (172 before), into a 400-word one 172 (116), a 30-word one into 400 words 198 (133), and 60 into 600 words 200 (135). The 28 misses of 15 into 400 are all a sketch holding none of the short scene's 13 hashes up to τ (about 64 / 413 of the range, so none about 11% of the time): with 64 values there is nothing to go on then, and a larger sketch is the only cure, so it stays a known limit. The 13 labelled pairs and the 276 different ones are all still right. **Two shingles in common under one heading** (the second ruling): "The phone rings." and "The phone stops." share the pair "the phone", one of three shingles (C 0.33), so C ≥ 0.25 alone called them one scene; C × the smaller's shingles, rounded, must be at least 2 (or 1 for a scene of one shingle, so "They drive." still matches "They drive in silence."). It is exact for scenes that fit their sketches, and for larger ones C ≥ 0.25 of 8 or more shingles is already two. **Rule 2 is one-way in Deleted scenes** (the second ruling): `deletedScenes` asks `coveredBy(past, now)`, which is `sameScene` except that a scene now with no printed words covers only a past version with none. A heading written first (empty then, filled now) is still there; a scene emptied to its heading, or cut with a bare heading of the same name left (a ten-word `INT. CAR - NIGHT` scene with an empty `INT. CAR - NIGHT` now), is listed, so a placeholder never hides a cut. `sameScene` stays symmetric, and so do `sceneVersions` and `putBackPlace`, which use it: following a scene back through an emptied version, or putting one back after a neighbour that is now only its heading, is what the writer wants.
- **2026-10-30** — **Deleted is many to one, resolved once per version** (Calls 2 and 7): each distinct hash is looked up among the scenes now by hash, then against the scenes now with its heading key, then against all of them, once per call. A deleted version joins the entry whose newest or most recently added version it matches, else starts one; an entry counts each snapshot once (`versions`), keeps its newest version and when the oldest was taken, and is keyed by its newest version's hash. Newest deleted first, then by place. Both lookups go only to the candidates that could match, filed by hash, heading key, and every sketch value (each rule but the first two needs a sketch value in common, so none is missed, and between two entries that match, the first started still wins). **A version with words is looked for under its heading key among the empty versions only** (after the second review), since the heading rule needs one side empty; an empty version still looks among all under its key. Before, with the scaling history's headings reused (36 headings, as a script comes back to a few dozen places), each version under a heading was a candidate for every other, and 300 to 1,200 snapshots grew 7.0× (5.4 ms to 38.2 ms); now 5.7× (3.8 ms to 21.8 ms), the same as with every heading different (5.6×), and 4.0 ms for 300 snapshots. The reviewer's random histories (600 each, two seeds, a third of edits emptying a scene) give the same entries as the reference that tries every scene now. The first version looked through every entry for each deleted version, quadratic in deleted scenes, and its growth test hid it: its snapshots held a fixed few versions and deletions. The test's history now writes a new version in every snapshot and cuts a scene and adds one every third, so both grow with the snapshots, and 300 to 1,200 grows about 5× (11× before). In Node, the reviewer's histories: 300 snapshots of 150 scenes with a scene cut and one added in each, 17 ms (75 before); every third, 8 ms (21); 1,000 of 400 scenes, 57 ms (770) and 33 ms (234).
- **2026-10-30** — **Scene versions chain, merge, and go on past a gap** (Calls 28 and 29): in each snapshot, newest first, the version is the best match to the one found in the next newer snapshot (else the scene now): highest J (the same hash first), then the same heading, then the place nearest the last one found. A snapshot without one is a gap row, and consecutive gaps or consecutive equal hashes are one row.
- **2026-10-30** — **Put back is one insertion of the line breaks it needs** (Call 11): `putBackPlace` tries the old previous scene (after its match now) or, for a scene that opened a section, the old next one (before its match), then the other, then outwards, alternating, nearest first; a neighbour's match is one with its hash, else the closest that matches, nearest its old place. `putBackChanges` inserts the scene in the script's own line breaks and adds only the line breaks missing for a blank line on each side, so it never removes text; at the end, it goes after the last block, before the `/* drafter */` settings. A line of spaces and tabs counts as blank there, but not two spaces alone, which Fountain keeps as a line of a speech. **A section opener whose old next scene is gone opens its section again** (the controller's ruling after review): when a section heading of its old section's title is in the script now (`scenePrints(…).sections`, each with the end of its heading and synopsis; slots carry `sectionTitle`), it goes right after that heading, rather than after its old previous scene at the end of the act before. Property tests over the Outline's random scripts: the insertion is outside every scene now, adds exactly the scene's text, leaves every other scene as it was, and the scene is no longer deleted after.
- **2026-10-30** — **The history worker reads lazily, newest first, and streams** (Calls 9 and 10): `workers/history.worker.ts` is a shell around `workers/history-compute.ts`, whose logic sits behind `SnapshotSource` (list and read) and `PrintStore` (the cache) and is tested in Node with `fake-indexeddb` (the plan's Call 40). Listing reads keys only: the `projectId` index's primary keys, then a reverse key cursor on `createdAt` for the order (newest first, the larger id first on a tie, as History lists them), so no snapshot's text is loaded to list it. A snapshot is read and gunzipped only on a miss, and printed with `scenePrints(text, known)`, so a scene version already printed isn't shingled again. A worker also keeps what it read or the cache had for one script in memory, so matching again after typing reads nothing. An update (a progress reply, then the list as it stands) goes after the first snapshot and then at most every `STREAM_MS` = 100 ms; new rows go to the cache at each update; a full scan ends with the sweep. At most `MAX_SNAPSHOTS` = 1,000, newest first, with `capped` said. A row that can't be read (damaged, or gzip where there is no `DecompressionStream`) is skipped and counted; one deleted mid-scan is skipped and not counted.
- **2026-10-30** — **Cancellation by lane** (§3.4): each kind of scan (`deleted`, `versions`) has a lane, and a newer request in a lane stops the older at its next snapshot, so Deleted scenes and History of this scene, open together, don't stop each other. A cancel carries the number of the request it stops. The client (`history/client.ts`) is one worker for every view, made on a view's first request and ended when the last view releases; a request hears only its own replies until its last (or an error), a newer request of a kind drops the older's listener as the worker stops it, and release cancels that view's scans still running. A worker that errors, or that can't be made (a browser blocking module workers), answers each waiting request with an error.
- **2026-10-30** — **The prints cache** (Call 13, the controller's ruling): Dexie database `drafter-cache`, version 1, `prints` keyed `[projectId+hash]` (index `projectId`) and `snapshotScenes` keyed by the snapshot's `id` (index `projectId`), each row with its `version`; a row of another `PRINT_VERSION` is a miss, and a cached snapshot whose slots name a print the cache lacks is read again. Rows are read a script at a time (two index reads), so a warm scan does no per-snapshot lookups. The sweep, after every full scan: drop `snapshotScenes` rows whose snapshot is gone, then `prints` no remaining row names. `removeProject` clears the script's rows after its own transaction; a failure there is swallowed (the removal stands). Where the cache can't open, or any call to it fails, the scan goes on without it, every time, with the same results.
- **2026-10-30** — **The cache is kept to scripts that exist, and a stopped scan stores what it read** (after Task 3's review): a scan that is stopped (a newer request, a cancel) writes its read rows before it returns, so none wait in the worker's queue to be written by another script's scan after the first script is removed. A worker's first full scan also prunes the rows of every script no longer in `drafter` (`prune`, keys only), which catches a write that lands after `removeProject` cleared the cache. The sweep drops the script's rows of another `PRINT_VERSION` too, since they are never read again. A request that finds its script's memory loading keeps that memory, even if another script's request replaces the worker's memory before it has loaded.
- **2026-10-30** — **Firefox's matching again is 53 ms, over the 50 ms budget, accepted** (after Task 3's review): it is the same worker reading memory, on the Mac with five projects scanned at once; Chromium is 28 ms and WebKit 37. It is off the main thread, so a few milliseconds over make no long task, and the e2e fails only past ten times the budget.
- **2026-10-30** — **Listing walks every script's snapshot keys** (after Task 3's review): `dexieSource().list` takes the script's ids from the `projectId` index, then walks the whole `createdAt` index, keys only, to put them newest first. With many scripts that is every snapshot's key, a few milliseconds per thousand; it reads no text, and a compound `[projectId+createdAt]` index would need `drafter` version 2, which this release doesn't take (Call 13).
- **2026-10-30** — **The put-back properties are seeded** (after Task 3's review): a full run before Task 2's second fix round merged failed once; with it, 600,000 unseeded runs of each passed. They run 300 times from seed 20261030, as the similarity property does, so a failure is the same every time.
- **2026-10-30** — **Compare's Changes view** (version-history design §5, Calls 18 to 27): `compare/rows.ts`'s `diffModel` gives `diffRows` and `groupRows` the same anchors (a script's `sceneAnchors`, a Markdown document's `markdownAnchors`), so a change across a heading is cut there; its `changes` are the comparison's, so the Pages view's marks and the Changes view's stops are the same changes. A group with an empty `changes` list is not shown. The summary counts groups with a change by tag (the opening isn't a scene) and names a Markdown document's groups sections. A stop in the Changes view is named by its group's heading, "INT. HALL - DAY: 2 lines changed", or "Line 2: …" before the first heading; focus goes to the change's first row, opening its group if it was closed. **Go to scene** (`goToScene`) uses the heading's offset when the newer side is now and the text hasn't changed since Compare opened; else the scene now that `sameScene` matches, the same version first, then the same heading key, then the highest J, then the nearest ordinal; else it says the scene is gone; a deleted group's scene is looked for the same way, so a moved scene is found. A Markdown heading is found by its text. **Compare's layout worker starts only when the Pages view is first shown** (it isn't needed for Changes). `CompareLines.vue` is gone: the Changes view replaces it for a Markdown document and for a failed layout, where Pages says so. Rows are components drawn only for open groups; a group paints only on screen (`content-visibility: auto`); changed words are diffed when a pair is first drawn and kept. A fold's button names the lines it holds; opening one moves focus to the first line it showed. Side by side is a `<table>` per group with Before and After column headers (the versions' labels), each cell keeping its hidden "removed, " or "added, "; a fold is a row with one cell across both.
- **2026-10-30** — **The Pages view keeps its asterisks** (the controller's ruling on Call 19): an asterisk is the production convention for a revised line, and what Phase 4's printed marks will use, so Compare's Pages view keeps v0.13.0's asterisk and "N lines removed" buttons; the + and − with the diff colors are the Changes view's, and Show changes' (Task 5). `compare.spec.ts` chooses Pages first; `compare-changes.spec.ts` checks Pages still has its asterisks.
- **2026-10-30** — **The diff colors are Drafter's own, tested for contrast** (Calls 32 and 41): `compare/colors.ts`'s `DIFF_TOKENS`, two palettes in two themes, seven tokens each (row tints, word tints, signs, and the fold and header tint), drawn on the theme's `--page` with `--ink` and `--ink-muted`. `test/diff-colors.test.ts` checks text and line numbers on every tint at 4.5:1 or more (the lowest is 4.6, `--ink-muted` on a row tint in the light theme), the signs on their row tints and on the header tint at 4.5:1, the signs as bars and rules on the page at 3:1, and that `tokens.css` holds the same values. Red and green is the default on the theme's own selectors; Blue and orange by `data-diff-colors` on the root, which `settings/apply.ts` sets from `diffColors`. **Settings:** `diffColors` (`red-green`), `compareView` (`changes`), `diffLayout` (`unified`), each checked by `isValid`. Only Colors for changes is in the Settings dialog; the view and layout are chosen on Compare's slim bar and remembered.
- **2026-10-30** — **Axe runs on the Changes view** (the controller's ruling on Call 36): `compare-changes.spec.ts` checks it unified and, where there is room, side by side, in both themes and both palettes, on every project. The screen-reader listening pass still waits for the final steps.
- **2026-10-30** — **Measured** (e2e `a whole rewrite draws collapsed groups quickly`: two unrelated scripts of 5,000 lines, ten scenes each, the maintainer's Mac): from Enter in History to the summary and the closed groups drawn, 52 ms on Chromium, 125 ms Firefox, 207 ms WebKit, 80 ms and 47 ms on the phones. The headings of the two scripts are alike enough to pair, so each scene is one renamed group of 996 changed lines, closed. The test fails only past 5 s.

- **2026-10-30** — **Measured** (e2e `history-scan.spec.ts`, 300 snapshots of a 127-page script of invented words, each snapshot with one scene changed; the maintainer's Mac, the build served locally, five projects at once): cold scan, first rows 46 ms and all 300 in 1.2 s on Chromium (budgets 1 s and 10 s); WebKit 70 ms and 1.4 s; Firefox 63 ms and 4.6 s; the phones 51 ms / 1.1 s (Chromium) and 61 ms / 1.1 s (WebKit). Warm scan, a new worker reading the cache: 54 ms on Chromium (budget 500 ms), WebKit 67, Firefox 89. Matching again after an edit, the same worker: 28 ms on Chromium (budget 50 ms), WebKit 37, Firefox 53. Typing during a cold scan made no long task on Chromium or the Chromium phone.
